     359                         : 
     360                         :     void EndStructLayout(UniformLayoutInfo* ioStructInfo) override
     361         7686            :     {
     362         7686            :         SLANG_UNUSED(ioStructInfo);
     363                         : 
     364                         :         // Note: A traditional C layout algorithm would adjust the size
     365                         :         // of a struct type so that it is a multiple of the alignment.
     366                         :         // This is a parsimonious design choice because it means that
     367                         :         // `sizeof(T)` can both be used when copying/allocating a single
     368                         :         // value of type `T` or an array of N values, without having to
     369                         :         // consider more details.
     370                         :         //
     371                         :         // Of course the choice also has down-sides in that wrapping things
     372                         :         // into a `struct` can affect layout in ways that waste space. E.g.,
     373                         :         // the following two cases don't lay out the same:
     374                         :         //
     375                         :         //      struct S0 { double d; float f; float g; };
     376                         :         //
     377                         :         //      struct X  { double d; float f; }
     378                         :         //      struct S1 { X x;               float g; }
     379                         :         //
     380                         :         // Even though `S0::g` and `S1::g` have the same amount of useful
     381                         :         // data in front of them, they will not land at the same offset,
     382                         :         // and the resulting struct sizes will differ (`sizeof(S0)` will be
     383                         :         // 16 while `sizeof(S1)` will be 24).
     384                         :         //
     385                         :         // Slang doesn't get to be opinionated about this stuff because
     386                         :         // there is already precedent in both HLSL and GLSL for types
     387                         :         // that have a size that is not rounded up to their alignment.
     388                         :         //
     389                         :         // Our default layout rules won't implement the C-like policy,
     390                         :         // and instead it will be injected in the concrete implementations
     391                         :         // that require it.
     392         7686            :     }
     393                         :

     527                         : struct HLSLConstantBufferLayoutRulesImpl : DefaultLayoutRulesImpl
     528                         : {
     529                         :     typedef DefaultLayoutRulesImpl Super;
     530                         : 
     531                         :     // Similar to GLSL `std140` rules, an HLSL constant buffer requires that
     532                         :     // `struct` and array types have 16-byte alignement.
     533                         :     //
     534                         :     // Unlike GLSL `std140`, the overall size of an array or `struct` type
     535                         :     // is *not* rounded up to the alignment, so it is possible for later
     536                         :     // fields to sneak into the "tail space" left behind by a preceding
     537                         :     // structure or array. E.g., in this example:
     538                         :     //
     539                         :     //     struct S { float3 a[2]; float b; };
     540                         :     //
     541                         :     // The stride of the array `a` is 16 bytes per element, but the size
     542                         :     // of `a` will only be 28 bytes (not 32), so that `b` can fit into
     543                         :     // the space after the last array element and the overall structure
     544                         :     // will have a size of 32 bytes.
     545                         : 
     546                         :     SimpleArrayLayoutInfo GetArrayLayout(SimpleLayoutInfo elementInfo, LayoutSize elementCount)
     547                         :         override
     548          159            :     {
     549          159 (2/2)      :         if (elementInfo.kind == LayoutResourceKind::Uniform)
     550          100            :         {
     551          100            :             elementInfo.alignment = maximum(elementInfo.alignment, 16);
     552          100            :         }
     553          159            :         return Super::GetArrayLayout(elementInfo, elementCount);
     554          159            :     }
     555                         : 
     556                         :     SimpleLayoutInfo GetPointerLayout(const TypeLayoutContext&) override
     557            4            :     {
     558                         :         // Not supported on HLSL currently...
     559            4            :         return SimpleLayoutInfo();
     560            4            :     }
     561                         : 
     562         6833            :     UniformLayoutInfo BeginStructLayout() override { return UniformLayoutInfo(0, 16); }
     563                         : 
     564                         :     // HLSL layout rules do *not* impose additional alignment
     565                         :     // constraints on vectors (e.g., all of `float`, `float2`,
     566                         :     // `float3`, and `float4` have 4-byte alignment), but instead
     567                         :     // they impose a rule that any `struct` field must not
     568                         :     // "straddle" a 16-byte boundary.
     569                         :     //
     570                         :     // This has the effect of making it *look* like `float4`
     571                         :     // values have 16-byte alignment in practice, but the
     572                         :     // effects on `float2` and `float3` are more nuanched and
     573                         :     // lead to different result than the GLSL rules.
     574                         :     //
     575                         :     LayoutSize AddStructField(UniformLayoutInfo* ioStructInfo, UniformLayoutInfo fieldInfo) override
     576          838            :     {
     577                         :         // Skip zero-size fields
     578          838 (1/2)      :         if (fieldInfo.size.compare(0) == std::partial_ordering::equivalent)
     579            0            :             return ioStructInfo->size;
     580                         : 
     581          838            :         ioStructInfo->alignment = maximum(ioStructInfo->alignment, fieldInfo.alignment);
     582          838            :         ioStructInfo->size = _roundToAlignment(ioStructInfo->size, fieldInfo.alignment);
     583                         : 
     584          838            :         LayoutSize fieldOffset = ioStructInfo->size;
     585          838            :         LayoutSize fieldSize = fieldInfo.size;
     586                         : 
     587                         :         // Would this field cross a 16-byte boundary?
     588          838            :         UInt registerSize = 16;
     589          838            :         auto startRegister = fieldOffset / registerSize;
     590          838            :         auto endRegister = (fieldOffset + fieldSize - 1) / registerSize;
     591          838 (2/2)      :         if (startRegister.compare(endRegister) != std::partial_ordering::equivalent)
     592          145            :         {
     593          145            :             ioStructInfo->size = _roundToAlignment(ioStructInfo->size, registerSize);
     594          145            :             fieldOffset = ioStructInfo->size;
     595          145            :         }
     596                         : 
     597          838            :         ioStructInfo->size += fieldInfo.size;
     598          838            :         return fieldOffset;
     599          838            :     }
     600                         : };

    3925                         :     // Computing the layout is made tricky by several factors.
    3926                         :     //
    3927                         :     // A parameter group has to draw a distinction between the element type,
    3928                         :     // and the resources it consumes, and the "container," which main
    3929                         :     // consume other resources. The type of resource consumed by
    3930                         :     // the two can overlap.
    3931                         :     //
    3932                         :     // Consider:
    3933                         :     //
    3934                         :     //      struct MyMaterial { float2 uvScale; Texture2D albedoMap; }
    3935                         :     //      ParameterBlock<MyMaterial> gMaterial;
    3936                         :     //
    3937                         :     // In this example, `gMaterial` will need both a constant buffer
    3938                         :     // binding (to hold the data for `uvScale`) and a texture binding
    3939                         :     // (for `albedoMap`). On Vulkan, those two things require the *same*
    3940                         :     // `LayoutResourceKind` (representing a GLSL `binding`). We will
    3941                         :     // thus track the resource usage of the "container" type and
    3942                         :     // element type separately, and then combine these to form
    3943                         :     // the overall layout for the parameter group.
    3944                         : 
    3945                         :     // Note: We leave the `type` field of the container type layout
    3946                         :     // as null, because there is no available `Type*` that is suitable
    3947                         :     // to put there.
    3948                         :     //
    3949                         :     // It might seem like we could use the `parameterGroupType` itself,
    3950                         :     // since it is the logical "container," but doing so creates an
    3951                         :     // unfortunate situation where we have a layout for a parameter
    3952                         :     // group type that is not itself a `ParameterGroupTypeLayout`.
    3953                         :     // Furthermore, it creates a nesting situation where a type layout
    3954                         :     // for `parameterGroupType` contains a nested layout for `parameterGroupType`,
    3955                         :     // and thus creates the impression of an infinite regress.
    3956                         :     //
    3957                         :     // The down-side to leaving a null pointer here is that it means
    3958                         :     // we do *not* have an invariant that every `TypeLayout` has a non-null
    3959                         :     // type, but that property is not explicitly useful/desirable.
    3960                         : 
    3961        14600            :     RefPtr<TypeLayout> containerTypeLayout = new TypeLayout();
    3962        14600            :     containerTypeLayout->rules = parameterGroupRules;
    3963                         : 
    3964                         :     // Because the container and element types will each be situated
    3965                         :     // at some offset relative to the initial register/binding for
    3966                         :     // the group as a whole, we allocate a `VarLayout` for both
    3967                         :     // the container and the element type, to store that offset
    3968                         :     // information (think of `TypeLayout`s as holding size information,
    3969                         :     // while `VarLayout`s hold offset information).
    3970                         : 
    3971        14600            :     RefPtr<VarLayout> containerVarLayout = new VarLayout();

    3977        14600            :     typeLayout->elementVarLayout = elementVarLayout;
    3978                         : 
    3979                         :     // It is possible to have a `ConstantBuffer<T>` that doesn't
    3980                         :     // actually need a constant buffer register/binding allocated to it,
    3981                         :     // because the type `T` doesn't actually contain any ordinary/uniform
    3982                         :     // data that needs to go into the constant buffer. For example:
    3983                         :     //
    3984                         :     //      struct MyMaterial { Texture2D t; SamplerState s; };
    3985                         :     //      ConstantBuffer<MyMaterial> gMaterial;
    3986                         :     //
    3987                         :     // In this example, the `gMaterial` parameter doesn't actually need
    3988                         :     // a constant buffer allocated for it. This isn't something that
    3989                         :     // comes up often for `ConstantBuffer`, but can happen a lot for
    3990                         :     // `ParameterBlock`.
    3991                         :     //
    3992                         :     // To determine if we actually need a constant-buffer binding,
    3993                         :     // we will inspect the element type and see if it contains
    3994                         :     // any ordinary/uniform data *or* any interface/existential-type
    3995                         :     // slots.
    3996                         :     //
    3997                         :     // The latter detail might sound surprising, because it means
    3998                         :     // that for a declaration like:
    3999                         :     //
    4000                         :     //      cbuffer U { IThing gThing; }

    4002                         :     // we will allocate a constant-buffer binding for `U` whether
    4003                         :     // or not it turns out that the concrete type plugged in for
    4004                         :     // `IThing gThing` has any ordinary/uniform data at all (that is,
    4005                         :     // if the user plugs in a type that only holds a `Texture2D`,
    4006                         :     // we will still have allocated the constant buffer binding/register,
    4007                         :     // and waste it on an empty buffer).
    4008                         :     //
    4009                         :     // The reason for this choice is that it greatly simplifies
    4010                         :     // logic for clients of Slang: a given `ConstantBuffer<>` or

    4012                         :     // need a constant buffer binding or not, based on its declared

    4014                         :     // that (e.g., plugging in a new value/object for `gThing`

    4569                         : // Given the type layout for an element of an array, apply any adjustments required
    4570                         : // based on the element count of the array.
    4571                         : //

    4573                         : // type that contains resources, since each resource field might need to be at
    4574                         : // a different offset than we would otherwise expect.
    4575                         : //
    4576                         : // For example, given:
    4577                         : //
    4578                         : //      struct Foo { Texture2D a; Texture2D b; }

    4582                         : //      Foo foo;

    5950                         :             // to hold a value of "any" type, with the expectation that:
    5951                         :             //
    5952                         :             // * Values which fit entirely in the storage we've reserved
    5953                         :             //   will be stored there directly.

    5955                         :             // * Values that are too big to store directly will be referenced
    5956                         :             //   indirectly, by a pointer stored in the reserved space.

    5959                         :             // reservation must be large enough to store a pointer.

    5962                         :             // or not specialization is being used, because we do not
    5963                         :             // want host code that sets parameters to have to be re-run (and
    5964                         :             // behave differently) depending on whether specialization is
    5965                         :             // being used for a particular dispatch.
    5966                         :             //
    5967                         :             // For target platforms that do not support flexible memory
    5968                         :             // addressing, we can follow the same approach in cases
    5969                         :             // where a value fits in the reserved memory space, and we
    5970                         :             // will discuss what happens in the other cases in a bit.
    5971                         :             //
    5972                         :             // The default reservation will be 16 bytes (and this number
    5973                         :             // becomes part of our ABI contract), but the `interface`
    5974                         :             // that is being used to bound the existential can have
